| F153 | **Medium-High — real, recurring, quantified across 54 days; not the day's fluke David suspected, worse and more frequent than that** | David, direct via David's Desk, flagging today's watch-reported +131ft climb over miles 2-5 as implausible for a route with progressively-faster splits ("this has been an issue on the watch before"). Code agent investigated on real data, not assumed: today's run exists as TWO independently-computed rows — `watch` (live, on-device, 131ft) and `apple_watch` (post-run, HealthKit-derived, 38ft) — same physical run, 3.45x apart. **Not isolated to today**: pulling all 54 paired days back to May, 43 agree exactly, but the 11 that diverge do so by up to 13x, in EITHER direction, and every one of the last 6 running days diverges — recently more frequent, not a one-off. **Root cause confirmed on today's own per-mile data**: `WorkoutTracker.swift` (watch) nets barometric altitude over ~0.1mi GPS segments for a live-updating stat; `HealthKitImporter.swift` (phone) nets per completed MILE, ~6x coarser. Finer netting means more segment boundaries, each an independent chance for one-sided barometric jitter to read as fictional climb — the watch's own code comment already documents a prior 2026-08-28 fix for a worse instance of this exact class. Today's `apple_watch` per-mile deltas (+12/-17/-13/+20/+6/-4) are the textbook noise oscillation signature, summing exactly to the stored 38ft total — confirms no real hill, route is close to flat. **Why the app shows 131, not 38**: `lib/runs/canonical.ts`'s `SOURCE_TIER` ranks `watch` above `apple_watch` as a blanket per-source policy (correct for HR/cadence/distance, where the watch's live sensor reads genuinely are more trustworthy) — but for `elevGainFt` specifically, the "preferred" source is structurally the noisier algorithm, and the picker has no field-specific awareness of that. **Correctly NOT fixed unilaterally**: flipping `SOURCE_TIER` wholesale would break every other field it correctly governs. Three real options identified, none picked: (A) prefer the coarser phone-import computation for `elevGainFt` specifically; (B) apply the already-shipped `elev-sanity.ts` cross-corroboration logic between the two independently-computed totals, not just within one source's own splits; (C) raise `WorkoutTracker.swift`'s own noise floor or widen its netting segment to close the gap at the source. Correctly framed as the same shape of decision as F139's tradeoff — a real design/doctrine call, not a mechanical bug fix. Separately, part 1 of the same dispatch (spot-checking whether recorded-but-less-visible metrics are actually used): `avgPowerW`/`avgGctMs`/`avgVertOscCm`/`avgStrideLengthM` all confirmed genuinely consumed by `health-state.ts`/`load-activity-evidence.ts`/`recovery-phase.ts`/`coherence.ts`, not orphaned — honestly disclosed as a spot check on likely-write-only fields, not an exhaustive audit. | **RULED, coach consultant, 2026-09-15: Option B, not A. C is real but a separate follow-on, not today's answer.** Self-corrected the routing framing first, honestly: this isn't F139's shape (a real product-doctrine document) — it's sensor-accuracy engineering, a narrower call. **Found the actual pre-existing resolver mechanism that should have caught this**: `elevation.ts#pickElevationGain`, already built after a near-identical prior incident (128ft canonical vs. 13ft barometric twin) — it ranks by SOURCE LABEL (raw/barometric > gps_derived), but today's failure is a genuinely different mechanism than the one it was built for: not sensor type, but netting RESOLUTION (watch nets fine 0.1mi segments, compounding jitter; phone nets per-mile, letting jitter cancel). Same source label both times — the resolver was never asked this question. **Why not A (blanket-prefer-phone)**: swaps in device identity as the trust axis, which this file's OWN doctrine already explicitly rejected once ("provenance belongs to the family, not the field") — risks silently reintroducing the ORIGINAL GPS-derived bug pointed the other way, for whichever future case the phone's own figure happens to be the weaker one. **Why B**: extends `elev-sanity.ts`'s own already-established discipline (demand corroboration before trusting a suspicious value) across sources instead of just within one source's own splits — closes the actual observed harm (11 of 54 paired days, up to 13x, every one of the last 6 days) without a device-identity shortcut. C (recompute server-side from raw samples at better netting resolution) is the real root-cause fix but bigger — valuable follow-up, not blocking. **Independent bonus finding, not asked for but real: `elev-sanity.ts`'s own header cites "Research/12" for its 250 ft/mi threshold — Research/12 is travel/timezone management, not course-specific training (that's Research/11), and the 250 ft/mi figure isn't actually in Research/11 either. A real citation defect, worth fixing regardless of the A/B/C call, logged separately as F154.** | — | David, direct via David's Desk, 2026-09-15; investigated with real 54-day data, code agent, same day; ruled (Option B), coach consultant, `for coaching consult/consult-log/2026-09-15-042-run-debrief-final-and-f153-elevation-source.md` |

| F157 | **CRITICAL — live, active, ongoing production database pool exhaustion, real server logs not code reasoning; strong candidate for F156's actual root cause, potentially affecting other users right now, not just David** | Code agent, F156 investigation, pulled Railway's live production deployment logs directly (real-time stdout, not a point-in-time check or client-code reading): repeated `pg-pool` errors — "timeout exceeded when trying to connect," meaning the database connection pool could not get a free connection within its 10-second timeout. 47 of the last 295 log lines were this exact error, clustered in roughly the last 90 seconds at time of finding, confirmed STILL HAPPENING, not historical. **Explains the "different symptom each time" pattern precisely**: the same pool-exhaustion signature hit a completely unrelated endpoint (`plan-snapshot`, "race outlook resolution timeout... serving last-known-good projection") in the same log window — that endpoint has a graceful cache fallback, while `/api/v5/today` has none and fails outright as an "outage." Still holding — no new connections opened, pool config untouched, waiting for authorization. **David's explicit call, direct via David's Desk, 2026-09-15: diagnose further before touching the production pool size — he does NOT want it raised blind as a first move.** Not just "not yet authorized" — an actual stated preference for diagnosis-first over a quick config bump, given this touches production infra config that may need real infra judgment beyond a normal code fix. Standing instruction to the code agent: keep investigating (without adding DB load) to sharpen or falsify the over-subscription theory before proposing a specific remediation, rather than defaulting to "just raise `max`." **Structural analysis, code agent, 2026-09-15, zero new DB queries — a real, largely non-speculative answer to "is this us or pre-existing."** Two facts from config/code alone: (1) `railway.json` has no replica/scaling config, so Railway defaults to a single production instance — `pool.ts`'s `max: 8` is very likely a hard ceiling for the ENTIRE app (every user, every route, every cron), not 8-per-something-larger. Tonight's diagnostics may have added marginal pressure on top, not ruled out, but no longer the primary suspect. **One remaining piece of uncertainty, and it needs a human with dashboard access, not more code reading**: which specific cron(s) were actually running during the exact timeout window is not determinable from source code alone — needs either Railway's own connection-count metrics graph or live cron-schedule visibility, both of which a human with Railway dashboard access could check directly and immediately, faster than further source-code reasoning. **Concrete, actionable next step: someone with Railway dashboard access should pull the connection-count graph for the last 30-60 minutes** — a sustained high baseline would confirm the structural (cron+traffic) theory; a sharp isolated spike would point back toward a burst of concurrent activity instead. **Auth/proxy ruled out, code agent, zero new queries — clean single-mechanism picture, not a mix of causes.** Grepped both saved log windows for every 401/unauthorized/session-expired/token-expired signature and every proxy/gateway/CDN signature (502/503/504, cloudflare, x-cache) — zero hits of either. Extracted all 16 scheduled workflows' cron expressions. Almost all (12 of them) run once daily between 03:00-10:00 UTC — nowhere near today's incident window (15:12-15:31 UTC), ruled out as the explanation. **But two are NOT once-daily and were squarely active during the exact incident window**: `tick.yml` runs every 10 minutes, all day, every day, forever — and its route is a META-DISPATCHER that queries a "last success" ledger for essentially every other cron job on EVERY single run, real DB work every 10 minutes regardless of whether anything is actually due to fire; `strava-push-poll.yml` runs every 15 minutes during 14:00-23:00 UTC, doing its own real per-user queries. **This is a PERMANENT, YEAR-ROUND structural pattern, not a tonight-specific spike**: a dispatcher firing every 10 minutes plus a real poller firing every 15 minutes for 9 hours a day, both sharing the same 8-connection pool with live user traffic, 24/7, since whenever these jobs were added. That baseline exposure has been there the whole time and surfaces as a visible failure whenever real load happens to line up with one of these frequent windows — precisely explaining David's own "it's been an issue in different ways for awhile now" and the earlier finding that different endpoints fail differently (fallback vs. no fallback) depending on which one is unlucky enough to need a connection at the wrong moment. **Concrete, prioritized, safety-ordered recommendation, in sequence, none executed**: (1) FIRST, before any pool-size number gets picked — someone with the Railway Postgres dashboard reads the database's actual `max_connections` ceiling; raising the app's pool blind past what the DB can actually serve would just move the failure point from "app can't get a client" to "DB refuses the connection entirely," exactly the blind change David is right to want avoided — a five-second dashboard read, not a database query. | F158 | **HIGH — a real, traceable, three-party-verified mechanism by which F152's own fix may be causing the "worse than ever" request-storm symptom; genuinely the most concrete lead found on F156 so far, still not fully confirmed against David's actual behavior** | **Code agent self-reported this immediately and directly, uncomfortable finding, not sat on.** Before F152, tapping Retry on a stuck card was a complete no-op. After F152 (which correctly made Retry actually functional, per its own confirmed fix), every Retry tap calls `goTo(date, force: true)`, which unconditionally fires `prefetchAround(iso)` (visible week + full prev/next week, ~21 days) and, when the day isn't cached, `fetchAndCacheWeek` (4 concurrent week-level fetches). Code agent's FIRST framing (later corrected, see below): claimed these week-level fetches were "raw, ungated, not deduplicated," meaning rapid repeated taps could each independently spawn a fresh ~21-day fan-out, plausibly compounding into the 120+ requests across ~18 dates David's own request log showed. **External reviewer independently checked this specific factual claim against the actual code before it got built on — found it WRONG, correctly caught before it misdirected a fix.** `fetchAndCacheWeek` genuinely has a cache-hit check AND a real, `@MainActor`-protected per-date in-flight guard (`weekFetchInFlight`) — simultaneous rapid-fire taps would NOT each spawn independent bursts; the guard blocks that. **Refined, more precise mechanism, external reviewer**: the real storm shape is SEQUENTIAL retries spaced past each attempt's own ~12-second client timeout — the in-flight guard clears on completion (success OR failure, via `defer`), not on start, so a runner re-tapping each time the error reappears (not mashing) still legitimately reopens a fresh ~21-day fan-out every time, because by the time they tap again the prior attempt has already timed out and released the guard. This exactly matches how a frustrated person actually behaves: tap, wait, see it's still broken, tap again. **Code agent independently re-verified the reviewer's correction against the code directly rather than just accepting it** — confirmed both guards are real (`weekCache`-range check, `weekFetchInFlight` clearing on `defer`, not on start) and the refined sequential-retry shape is correct and more plausible than the original tap-mashing framing. | **Proposed fix, deliberately NOT implemented yet — code agent explicitly declined to ship solo given tonight's pattern, asked for review first.** A cooldown was considered and correctly rejected by the external reviewer's own reasoning: anything shorter than the ~12s network timeout wouldn't help, anything longer would make a genuinely-recovered connection feel unresponsive. **Actual proposed shape**: `retryPending` shouldn't re-trigger the full ~21-day `prefetchAround`/4-week `fetchAndCacheWeek` fan-out at all — a retry of one specific stuck day is fundamentally different from a fresh navigation, and that broader prefetch already ran once when the day was first navigated to. Give `retryPending` a narrower path that retries only the specific day's own fetch (through `navigationTask`, unchanged) without re-firing the week-level prefetch machinery. **Needs review of the fix SHAPE before any code changes**, per the code agent's own correct instinct. **Still not fully confirmed against reality — one concrete check pending**: ask David directly how many times he actually tapped Retry during the repro, and/or check the request log's own timestamp spacing (if the ~18-date burst clusters into groups roughly ~12+ seconds apart, that's the sequential-retry signature; if genuinely simultaneous, the mechanism above doesn't fully explain it). This is the strongest, most specific lead on F156 to date — stronger than the generic "method gap"/stale-code hypothesis raised earlier, though that hypothesis isn't yet ruled out either and may be a contributing factor alongside this one, not necessarily instead of it. Also confirmed, separately: the on-device request-diagnostics log is in-memory only, capped at 300 entries, no remote log-shipping path exists — screenshots/manual relay from David's device remain the only way to see it; building remote shipping would be real, separate engineering work. **Design reviewed and implemented, 2026-09-15.** External reviewer proposed the surgical fix shape (`prefetchAround`'s `includeWeekFetches` param, gating only the 4-way week fetch, day-level radius left untouched since `fetchCoordinator` already bounds/dedupes it) rather than a coarser skip-everything flag. Code agent checked the one correctness question raised (does anything read `weekSummary(for:)` as a retry side effect) directly: single call site, `.loading`-phase-only, cosmetic-only fallback in the worst case. Implemented exactly as designed: `goTo`'s new `skipWeekPrefetch` param, only `retryPending` passes `true`, confirmed by reading all 8 `goTo` call sites. `xcodebuild test`: 566/566 pass. Submitted as `RR-20260914-052` (preflight-clean, READY) for the external reviewer's own requested Rule 18 falsification. **NOT confirmed against the actual incident** — still waiting on the tap-count/timestamp fact David's Desk was asked to get; this fix is not to be treated as closing F151/F156/F158 until that lands, whatever it shows. **RULED OUT AS THE PRIMARY EXPLANATION, David, direct via David's Desk, verbatim: "no because I dontl always click retry and it usually just fails right away regardless of if I click retry or not."** The failure happens on first load, no Retry tap required — this cleanly rules out the retry-storm mechanism as THE explanation for David's core complaint. **Still worth fixing as a real, independently-verified secondary bug** (RR-20260914-052 stands on its own technical merits), but it is not the headline and should not be presented as resolving F151/F156. **Separately, the stale-build/CDN-cache hypothesis was directly ruled out, code agent, live check**: fresh `curl` requests to the exact production domain at the exact live commit showed no CDN/proxy layer anywhere in the path (Railway's own edge only, no Cloudflare/third-party), two consecutive identical requests got two different server-generated correlation IDs (proving live, uncached processing each time), no `Cache-Control`/`Age`/`X-Cache` headers anywhere. Combined with David's own on-device request log showing real durations and real outcomes: a fresh request from his device genuinely reaches the current deployed code every time — "the fix never actually reached him" is cleanly eliminated as an explanation for any of the four misses. **RULED OUT AS THE CORE EXPLANATION, David direct via David's Desk, 2026-09-15: "I don't always click retry and it usually just fails right away regardless of if I click retry or not."** Failures happen on FIRST LOAD, with zero Retry taps — this cannot be explained by a retry-triggered re-fetch storm, which requires at least one retry to exist at all. ## Adding a finding

New findings get the next `F0NN` number in sequence, regardless of which review round found them.
Never reuse a number. Create `ER-20260914-F0NN.md` for anything needing more than a one-line
summary; the table row always gets updated regardless.
